2.

on
 
NEC
 
Bylaws
 
Per
 
the
 
NEC
 
Bylaws,
 
the
 
committee’s
 
bylaws
 
must
 
be
 
reviewed
 
every
 
three
 
years.
 
The
 
NEC
 
Bylaws
 
Subcommittee
 
was
 
formed
 
in
 
July
 
2025
 
to
 
review
 
the
 
bylaws
 
and
 
provide
 
recommendations.
 
Suggestions
 
were
 
also
 
collected
 
through
 
the
 
NEC
 
Survey
 
and
 
Future
 
Planning
 
Workshop.
 
Members
 
reviewed
 
the
 
changes
 
to
 
the
 
NEC
 
Bylaws
 
at
 
the
 
March
 
2026
 
NEC
 
meeting
 
and
 
were
 
provided
 
a
 
ﬁnal
 
draft
 
of
 
the
 
updated
 
bylaws
 
in
 
April
 
2026.

Barber
 
made
 
a
 
motion
 
to
 
approve
 
the
 
NEC
 
Bylaws.
 
Megan
 
von
 
Wedell
 
seconded
 
the
 
motion.
 
The
 
NEC
 
Bylaws
 
were
 
approved
 
unanimously.

Agenda Item #1.
Page 5 of 21
E.
 
Ollig
 
explained
 
that
 
Overland
 
Park
 
has
 
a
 
“mayor-council-city
 
manager”
 
form
 
of
 
government.
 
The
 
Council
 
has
 
the
 
authority
 
to
 
set
 
policy
 
direction
 
for
 
the
 
City
 
and
 
approve
 
the
 
budget
 
and
 
capital
 
improvements
 
program.
 
The
 
City
 
Manager
 
functions
 
as
 
the
 
chief
 
administrative
 
ofﬁcer
 
and
 
reports
 
to
 
the
 
Governing
 
Body,
 
while
 
the
 
various
 
departments
 
report
 
to
 
the
 
City
 
Manager.
 
The
 
role
 
of
 
staff
 
is
 
to
 
advise
 
the
 
Council
 
on
 
policy
 
options
 
and
 
execute
 
the
 
policy
 
direction
 
determined
 
by
 
the
 
Council.
 
a.
 
This
 
creates
 
a
 
working
 
relationship
 
where
 
the
 
council
 
members
 
don’t
 
get
 
too
 
far
 
into
 
the
 
details
 
of
 
operations,
 
allowing
 
staff
 
with
 
professional
 
expertise
 
in
 
business
 
areas
 
to
 
execute
 
the
 
City’s
 
programs
 
and
 
services.
 
 
b.
 
Council
 
members
 
help
 
set
 
the
 
priorities
 
and
 
goals
 
through
 
the
 
budget
 
process,
 
committee
 
work,
 
strategic
 
planning,
 
etc.
 
They
 
also
 
provide
 
leadership
 
and
 
political
 
accountability,
 
and
 
ensure
 
the
 
will
 
of
 
their
 
constituents
 
is
 
represented.
 
 
The
 
Governing
 
Body
 
is
 
composed
 
of
 
the
 
Mayor
 
and
 
12
 
City
 
Council
 
members,
 
two
 
for
 
each
 
of
 
the
 
six
 
wards,
 
who
 
serve
 
staggered
 
4-year
 
terms.

what

you

Drew
 
Mitrisin,
 
Ward
 
2
 
-
 
Councilmember
 
Mitrisin
 
indicates
 
that
 
parking
 
in
 
Downtown
 
Overland
 
Park
 
is
 
an
 
issue
 
because
 
it
 
is
 
free,
 
which
 
encourages
 
people
 
to
 
park
 
for
 
many
 
hours
 
of
 
the
 
day.
 
There
 
will
 
be
 
upcoming
 
conversations
 
to
 
discuss
 
how
 
to
 
keep
 
parking
 
turning
 
throughout
 
the
 
day,
 
so
 
more
 
people
 
can
 
beneﬁt.
 
Additionally,
 
as
 
land
 
is
 
developed
 
in
 
Downtown
 
Overland
 
Park,
 
City
 
Council
 
will
 
also
 
be
 
looking
 
for
 
opportunities
 
where
 
public-use
 
parking
 
spaces
 
can
 
be
 
added.
 
 
Councilmember
 
Mitrisin
 
noted
 
that

Park
 
Farmers

is
 
a
 
great
 
way
 
to

small
 
businesses.
 
Many

as

for

about

are

which
 
helps
 
attract

and

Q:
 
How
 
is
 
the
 
City
 
addressing
 
aging
 
infrastructure,
 
especially
 
in
 
our
 
older
 
neighborhoods?
 
How
 
are
 
priorities
 
determined
 
with
 
such
 
a
 
huge
 
city
 
to
 
maintain?
 
 
Andrew
 
Payne,
 
Ward
 
5
 
-
 
Councilmember
 
Payne
 
highlighted
 
the
 
City’s
 
long,
 
narrow
 
boundaries,
 
with
 
aging
 
infrastructure
 
from
 
the
 
north
 
to
 
the
 
south.
 
The
 
City
 
approved
 
the
 
OPMoves
 
⅜
 
cent
 
sales
 
tax
 
in
 
2023,
 
which
 
took
 
effect
 
in
 
April
 
2024.
 
The
 
⅜
 
cent
 
sales
 
tax
 
has
 
been
 
used
 
to
 
decrease
 
the
 
backlog
 
of
 
aging
 
infrastructure
 
such
 
as
 
roads,
 
curbs,
 
trafﬁc
 
signals,
 
stormwater,
 
and
 
sidewalks.
 
City
 
Council
 
uses
 
the
 
CIP
 
to
 
discuss
 
what
 
projects
 
will
 
be
 
prioritized
 
over
 
the
 
next
 
ﬁve
 
years,
 
and
 
the
 
CIP
 
is
 
re-evaluated
 
annually.
 
 
 
Drew
 
Mitrisin,
 
Ward
 
2
 
-
 
Councilmember
 
Mitrisin
 
emphasized
 
the
 
cost
 
of
 
infrastructure
 
projects,
 
the
 
massive
 
backlog
 
of
 
infrastructure
 
maintenance
 
and
 
the
 
complexities
 
of
 
tackling
 
the
 
backlog.
 
For
 
example,
 
a
 
single
 
trafﬁc
 
signal
 
costs
 
$1
 
million.
 
While
 
the
 
OPMoves
 
sales
 
tax
 
allows
 
Overland
 
Park
 
to
 
maintain
 
infrastructure
 
such
 
as
 
street
 
maintenance,
 
there
 
are
 
131
 
lane
 
miles
 
of
 
residential
 
streets
 
that
 
are
 
beyond
 
their
 
useful
 
life.
 
The
 
City
 
can
 
only
 
replace
 
4-5
 
lane
 
miles
 
annually,
 
which
 
costs
 
$50,000-$70,000,
 
and
 
projects
 
cannot
 
be
 
planned
 
out
 
too
 
far
 
in
 
advance
 
because
 
of
 
cost
 
changes.
 
In
 
addition
 
to
 
infrastructure
 
projects
 
being
 
expensive,
 
they
 
also
 
come
 
with
 
other
 
difﬁculties
 
such
 
as
 
disruption
 
and
 
tree
 
loss.
